ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e118	The theme that we address in this matter is not very controversial. An overall committee underlines the desire to put in place good schemes to stimulate the development of environmental technologies in Norway. We have many advantages that make this interesting for the nation to bet on, because we have good industrial clusters, good technology clusters, which also have capital available and – let's say – the right mentality to want to develop better environmental technologies in different areas. Nor does Norway lack good ideas, it turns out. We have many small entrepreneurs and large companies working in that direction, who see the potential in both large and small products of local and global character. It is a wish of an overall committee to help them develop their product for the commercial market. Then there is some disagreement in the committee on how to proceed, but there is good support for the work that is going on and that the Minister for the Environment has written to the Committee, and one looks forward to it being presented to the Stortinget on a case, so that we get a good debate and decided on good instruments. The opposition points out that today's schemes are not sufficient, because we often find that large companies do not have access to the funds in a way that raises the large projects. We also see that some small entrepreneurs point out that they like to get good help putting ideas down on the paper, but when you're going to go from the paper to test it out, and prove it to the market, it lacks it. On the other hand, in some cases the authorities are quite willing to help with the product when it is commercialised. But as some of these people have at least said to the undersigned, once the product has proven its function, the market arranges commercialization. It's not that someone needs the help of the authorities, but to prove that the product works. It is, of course, relevant to another issue dealt with in the Storting, namely a mechanism for the demonstration of wind turbines, which also shows that the actors believe in their products if only they are proved on the market. They don't think they need the help of the state to get into the market, but they have to prove it works. So an overall committee looks forward to the government's own case. Pending this, a majority of the Committee opposes the creation of its own fund for support for climate and environmental technologies. At the same time, as an opposition politician, I would like to stress what I have already said, that a system must facilitate participation in the aid regime by the large Norwegian industry. I would also like to stress the importance of Norwegian industrial and technology clusters for the future having framework conditions that enable them to continue their business here at home. We have seen, for example: compared to the climate quota law introduced by the government, that it is not attractive to invest in new industries in Norway, because the allocation of climate allowances is lower than if established in other countries. It cements today’s industrial structure in Norway but makes it difficult to be modern and develop if you don’t have a good home market. In addition, I would like to point out that in his letter to the Committee dated 28 April this year, the Minister for the Environment wrote that CO2 clean technology must be considered available. With the situation we now have, where CO2 treatment has been exposed in Mongstad for at least four years, and the Council of State in the Energy and Environmental Committee hearing last Thursday said that Norway is the most advanced in this, it is a bit strange to experience that, on the one hand, the letter in the Committee just a month ago states that technology is available, while three days later projects in Mongstad are postponed because the technology is still not available. It reduces the credibility of the government when it boasts of how good it is to develop environmental technologies, when the government itself completely misses a description of reality. I'm not going to go into the Mongstad case any more. It is moving on to the Committee on Energy and the Environment. But a proposition in terms of building confidence in the industry is that the government actually informs and has a correct picture of reality – they haven't had that long.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e147	Let me start by saying that in and of itself is a very nice suggestion right here comes with. We're all for climate technology. We're all to spend money on stimulating the development of it, even though it's not exactly very original to make all sorts of earmarked fund solutions out of the oil fund. That may well also be the reason why the Right is on its own about this proposal. Then there is already work in the government to develop a national strategy for environmental technologies. It is no more than a few weeks since it was announced that the government will spend 500 million. kr over three years to just new eco-innovation. We know that Norwegian business is far ahead. One of the freshest examples of this was when Statoil and Statkraft went ashore the giant contract for the development of sea wind at Doggerbank. After some reactions at the time, it would be thought that this was a national disaster, and that all Norwegian companies are doing should do so in Norway. For my part, it is absolutely amazing that our two heaviest energy companies have this focus – and not least the competence to win such contracts. Recently, I met people from a quite new company, GasPlas, who are driving the development of an entirely new technology in which CO2 is removed from natural gas in such a way that the carbon comes out as solid structures and can be used for everything from fertiliser to super-light and super-cheap carbon cycles – not CCS, but CCU, Carbon Capture and Use. And there are many other small and large examples of Norwegian companies that come up with amazing ideas. The greatest weakness we have is probably the transition between research and commercialisation. There is the rapporteur and I fully agree. It has to be the focus when the climate technology strategy is completed. So spending money on developing climate technologies is what we're all for. Then I have to say that when you read the setting, the big consensus about the reality description is perhaps the most striking. It says, for example. that we have ‘strong technology environments’. Many industries ‘have developed far within an environmentally friendly mindset’, and Norwegian ‘enterprises’ have a ‘mental’ competitive advantage when environmental challenges become an important part of the global competitive environment’. I'm 100 pst. agree with an overall committee here, but it is obvious to ask: why has it been so? Has it happened entirely by itself? There are many good, forward-looking actors in Norwegian business, but this is also the result of Norway's being in the forefront, with strict environmental legislation, strict requirements, early introduction of CO2 prices, a strict quota allocation. It's nice to spend money on what you like, like climate technology. However, if one is to get some way, the policy – the whole of the regime in which Norwegian business operates – must be linked. This is where the Right is failing, because as they talk about nice symbolic things – like what kind of energy label government building should have – in the environmental debates, they make wrong choices over and over again when it's going to make demands in those areas where it really matters to the emitters what we're doing. I remember very well all the debates we had on the Norwegian link to the European quota system, where the Right was one of the parties who cried out that the allocation was too strict – no demands were to be made on Norwegian companies beyond what was being made in Europe. But we see that it gives technology development. Hydro's new gas technology is a very good example. So I feel very sure that the story of Norway that had the strictest quota allocation in Europe is going to be exactly the same as the story of Norway that introduced the highest CO2 charge on Norwegian base. They made tough demands that stimulated a technology development that gave us an important competitive advantage when the rest of the world came after. Where's the right in this? We're not going beyond case number. 3 on today's map before we see that the Right is involved in a proposal to deviate from the CO2 treatment requirement for Norwegian gas plants. It's unclear to me what kind of technology development on the climate side they're going to have out of it. So here you go in the spagate on one and the same day – with just a little predatory discussion in the middle. To get back to carbon capture and storage and the most comprehensive climate technology project ever in this country: the opposition agreed to vote for test centre Mongstad last year. It's fine. But there was absolutely no ‘driving’ on CCS until the red-green government came in 2005. We've gone from nothing to being world leaders. One thing the opposition should have: without the red-green government, we wouldn't have any discussions about postponement in Mongstad, because we wouldn't have any CCS project at all. It is very close to mention that Jens Stoltenberg in the Questionnaire said that anyone who has no visions does not disappoint anyone either. The opposition is not even willing to guarantee that the Mongstad project will be followed up if we get a government shift, as the environmental movement asks them to do. One of the most important things the Right could do in this field was to ensure predictability of the largest climate technology project we have. Unfortunately, despite today's fine words about climate technology, it is among the ambitious Right.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e191	Let me start with the slightly overall perspective of why this proposal is an important and necessary proposal: the world needs more, not less, energy. 1.6 billion people today live without electricity. The International Energy Agency estimates that the world’s energy needs will increase by 45 pst. by 2030. At the same time, we need to cut greenhouse gas emissions by 80-90 pst. by 2050. Economic growth will help to lift hundreds of millions of people out of poverty – without degradation of biodiversity, without emissions to air, water and soil exceeding the resilience of nature and humans, without losing control of global warming. There are formidable challenges individually — and even more so when they are united. Solving them will require much of the human capacity for ingenuity and willingness to develop new technological solutions. The world must take a formidable climate promise in the future, and Norway must take part in this promise. We need companies and individuals that are stimulated to research, develop and commercialise new technological climate solutions. We need a flora of different approaches to one and the same problem complex, and we need companies that see new opportunities in green jobs and green technology development. Norway is pretty good at investing in research in climate-friendly technology. However, we are far behind other countries in commercialising research results in concrete products and processes. Therefore, we need a support system that helps to lift climate technologies from research results to commercialisation – a system that provides the opportunity to translate theory into practice, for example. through demonstration or pilot facilities for testing new technologies, a system which facilitates the exceeding of barriers in immature markets and significant technological risks. Today’s instrumentation in Enova and Innovation Norway is established for other purposes and has proved unsuitable for raising improved products and processes from research results in environmental technologies to businesses. Often, the companies in question become too large to be beneficiaries of support from today's tools. We therefore need to combine continued high research and development efforts with support and a better tool to take the projects from good ideas to commercialisation. Many different technologies may be involved in support from a climate technology fund. It could be, for example, be energy efficiency improvement and energy recovery in industry through the use of more efficient equipment such as electric motors, boilers, pumps and better integration of process facilities, the development of new renewable fuel and fuel to replace current fossil sources, process improvements in industry providing lower energy consumption per unit produced, or the development of substitute raw material for coke and coal in industrial processes – to mention something. The world’s sustainability is threatened by a significant market failure. It's too cheap to pollute and too expensive to be environmentally friendly. It is best solved through global agreements that increase the price of emissions, but technological developments cannot or should not wait for world leaders to agree on a higher carbon price. That is why we need a fund and an instrument for the development of climate-friendly technology. Change need not be the end of anything good. It might as well be the start of something better. This is also true of the changes that the climate crisis is driving. Norway has all the prerequisites — technology, competence and capital — to make the climate crisis an opportunity, not just a problem. But that requires that we play our cards right. In the right, investments in climate technology must be one of the most important strategic choices Norway can make for the future. Therefore, climate technology should become a fourth investment area for the use of oil money, alongside the areas of activity proposed by the Stoltenberg I Government in the context of the paper on the rules of action. There it appeared that the yield of the oil fund should go to growth-promoting tax relief – what is called voodoo policy by the Stoltenberg II Government – infrastructure such as road and rail and investment in knowledge and research. Climate and environmental technologies should enter this company of wise investments for the future. Finally, let me ask the representative Marthinsen a little visit. She pointed out in her post that the right hand side of this case was alone. That shows that the minority is often right. She also pointed out that the industry has done a lot to cut emissions on its own, among other things. through voluntary agreements, including those of the Right. She believes that the Right has little to boast about in climate policy. Well, we created CLIMIT. Already in 2002 we requested the clean-up of CO2 in the Bondevik II Climate Communication. I can mention the area by area where the Right has gone ahead, and where this government has voted down our initiative. I don't have to go further than to e.g. Our proposals for energy efficiency improvement, which the government does not take seriously, and we are now the only country in Europe with no clear energy efficiency improvement targets – to mention some examples. I would like, on behalf of the Right, to address the proposal contained in the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e276	Just a brief explanation of vote, given that we are not on the committee. For 2010, we doubled support for pilot plants in the environment, climate and energy sectors. We have also repeatedly proposed the creation of a semi-state investment company to provide risk capital for good climate change projects and for climate change entrepreneurs.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e548	The issue we are dealing with now concerns changes in the management of predators. Let's hope that the debate is also about predatory policies. Proposals seek to facilitate more flexible management of predatory animal policies and to facilitate more regional policy participation. Furthermore, the proposers request that stock targets be changed from being based on annual juveniles to count the number of actual individuals. The proposers will also be empowered by the Food Safety Authority to require the removal of pests in order to prevent animal trachea and that this should also apply to bovine animals and pure animals. Finally, the proposers ask the government to make the necessary changes to the game law in order to extend the emergency protection right to emergency workers in the event of predatory attacks against dogs and when predators act aggressively in the field. In 2004, the agreement on predatory animals was concluded between all batches on Stortinget, with the exception of the Progress Party and the Centre Party. The red-green government is basing its policy on this agreement. The predator agreement states that there will be 3 annual young wolves, 15 annual young bears, 39 annual young yerv, 65 annual young yerv and 858-1200 breeding pairs of queens. The agreement also provided for regional administration through six regions. This was an important instrument for the Bondevik II government, which presented predatory policies and to which the Stortinget agreed. We know that sometimes there can be a high level of conflict around predator management. That is why it is gratifying that a Joint Committee is also today fully supporting the 2004 Committee's position on quality of life. Let me quote: ‘The Committee considers that the quality of life and housing and the development of nutrition in rural areas must be taken into account in the management of predators. In the view of the Committee, the management must be carried out in a way that creates confidence between the local population and regional and central authorities.’.» There may be a high temperature in these debates. But I think that a debate like the one we have today can once again make us aware of whether there is a policy that is the right one. I'm glad. The attitude shows that today’s majority will not make any changes to predatory policies now. But at one point it is announced that one by the autumn of 2010 will have a review of the stock target for wolf and bear. The government also signals in the letter from Environment Minister Erik Solheim that they already want a broad settlement on this issue. The proposers want one to go from counting annual juveniles to counting the number of actual individuals. This does not give the majority of the Committee its assent, with different lines of argument. With regard to the proposal that decisions taken by predators should be final, the majority points to the legal certainty which lies in the possibility of challenging a decision. With regard to the proposal to empower the Food Safety Authority to require the removal of pests, the majority of the Committee points out that the management of wild game is the responsibility of the authorities responsible for predatory game and that the withdrawal of wild game must be in accordance with the laws of nature, wildlife and predatory regulations. The last proposal in the proposal for a representative, Document 8:95 S for 2009-2010, is to amend the game law in order to extend the emergency protection right. The majority of the Committee refers to the government statement at this point, where one should look at the Swedish experience of integrating a dog into the emergency guards regulations. The last seconds of my speaking time I will use to talk about the views of the Christian People's Party. We support the proposers in that the emergency protection right should be extended to dogs as well. We will in the time until the review of new stocking targets for wolves and bears assess whether we should move on to counting individuals. My final point in this article is that if we are to succeed in predator management in Norway, there must be a good interaction between central, regional and local levels of government. Interactions of this kind must be based on mutual respect in the conflicts and challenges that one at all times faces.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e581	The 2004 agreement on predators was an incredibly important step towards the management of the large predators in Norway which both ensure their survival and contribute to fewer conflicts and to a clearer framework for those who are to live and operate in areas where predators are present. The management of predators continues to be conflicty, and there are many heated discussions about what the predator agreement means and does not mean. But we should remember that the curtain back then, in 2004, was many years with enormously high levels of conflict and steep fronts. Three large-scale reports of predators had been received in ten years, and the aim was to arrive at a settlement that could stand over time, and that could create tranquility and predictability in predator management. Given how sensitive things are here, I believe that it has been quite successful. Now, six years later, we're there to review the stocking targets for bears and wolves, and we're going to evaluate the current land differentiation in the predatory game regions. However, the basic principles of the predatory agreement are established. The fact that these principles are still in place means that a good craft was done by those who negotiated the 2004 agreement on predators, although, naturally, we see disagreements in some individual cases. Proposals are subject to regional policy co-decision. So am I. The fact that predatory policies have credibility locally, where people actually live with predators, is crucial to respect laws and regulations and for good governance. The regional surveyors established in the wake of the predatory settlement shall ensure that this is the case. Those who are to implement predatory policies are those who can make management plans; they are those who are to allocate funds to anti-conflict measures and, in particular, set quotas for hunting and killing once the regional stock target has been reached. That decisions in the names can be appealed against, I believe, of course. It is a principle that applies in all parts of the administration, and it is important for legal certainty that there is an opportunity to complain. I think – with all due respect – that it is incredibly strange that the Progress Party is so dedicated to removing the appeal. It is obvious to ask whether this is the result of a principle approach to access to justice, and whether we are going to see proposals to remove it in other areas as well. But when that is said, I would like to say that there must be a very high threshold for the Ministry of the Environment to be able to re-approve decisions of the denominators. In rare cases — because rarely happens — there should be a heavy professional justification for this. At least equally important for the local anchoring, I think it's that people should be able to trust that the numbers of populations they're operating on are actually right. We see very well that disagreement between community and nature management about how many animals you actually have is a germ of distrust and makes good cooperation difficult. If you are constantly observing wolves in an area, but you are told by the administration that the animal does not exist, it is, of course, very difficult to trust that a stock target has not been reached. This is why we are concerned with both monitoring and stock registration and, in particular, increasing local participation. The DNA registration of bear, where local hunters are involved in the collection, is a very good example of how this can happen. In the proposal, representatives are asked to change the stock targets from being based on the number of annual juveniles to counting the number of actual individuals. When the number of juveniles is selected, it is mainly because it is easier to monitor than the number of individuals, and because it provides very important information about the development of predatory populations. However, there have been challenges – it is not possible to hide – especially in the case of bears, when we know that there have been many more male bears going around than the estimates based on the number of juveniles. This means that a new model has now been created for the calculation of juveniles of bears, which are better adapted to the situation in Norway. The question of dog emergency is a repeater in this room, and I assume that the opposition is very well aware that this comes as a separate matter to the Stortinget. In the case of emergency workers, it was emphasised by the opposition that this is a matter which must come as quickly as possible. Soria Moria II states that we need to look at the experiences that have been gained in Sweden. We all know that those experiences have been good. I think it is good that we have another opportunity to discuss the principles of predator management in Stortinget. It is good that there is a debate on these things, and I look forward to the round that will soon come where a new settlement will be negotiated on stock targets for wolves and bears.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e786	Of course, each such application and each such case will be considered. I therefore believe that it is a much more precise instrument to allow quick withdrawal when damage is done.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e878	It is not that changing CO2 handling requirements automatically leads to greater emissions. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e924	Our electricity supply is mainly based on hydropower. It makes us vulnerable, because within this energy system we depend on years of normal rainfall. Last year, this vulnerability has become visible. A cold and dry winter has made the filling density of the water reservoirs low, and we have imported electricity from our neighbouring country. However, the gas power plant at Kårstø has also contributed significantly to the power balance this year. Norway is a major exporter of natural gas to, inter alia: The continent. In fact, from a climate perspective, we are encouraging our neighbouring countries to make more use of gas to replace coal-fired power stations, since gas only has half of the carbon-fired climate emissions. In Europe, too, this form of energy is seen as an environmentally good energy production. In Norway, on the other hand, the government imposes requirements for CO2 cleaning from the first day for new gas power plants. This is considered to be so commercially demanding that in the foreseeable future we are unlikely to expect new gas power plants. The right-wing believe that this is regrettable. As has been said in debates earlier today, there is a contradiction between one on the one hand postponing Mongstad and cleaning at Mongstad, while the other on the other hand demands that cleaning technology be in place from day one in new gas power plants. The right is also concerned that we should invest in research and development to bring forward commercial gas power plant cleaning technologies. That is why we have supported, inter alia: the investment that is taking place in Mongstad. The difference is that the Right believes that we have to be able to make demands for cleaning arrangements, but that cleaning itself should not be a requirement from the start-up. Right is part of the climate agreement, and we are aware that unpurified concession means that cut greenhouse gas emissions will be more demanding overall. However, we want to strengthen our own balance of power by getting a larger total production, but at least equally important is that we get a more diversified production, where we do something more independent of the hydropower alone. In this winter, parts of Norway experienced very high electricity prices. The background is a combination of bottlenecks in the grid capacity, that Swedish nuclear power was out of production due to maintenance, and that winter was cold and low in rainfall. It is unfortunate that some parts of the country, including Middle Norway, will pay such a high price for a faulty energy policy. Both individuals and businesses have had to pay this price. This applies not only to existing businesses but also to the attractiveness of an area for new business start-ups. This means that energy availability, predictable prices and competitive prices are an important element. Therefore, the Right will support the Progress Party’s proposal to change the requirement for immediate CO2 cleaning in new gas power plants and ask the Government to make the same demands in Norway as in the EU. The question is what's important at the top. The global climate challenge is important, but the energy balance and ensuring a sufficient level of security of supply are also important for a society. The more gas we use for energy production, the less coal power we need to fire. This means that gas as a substitute for coal is a good environmental solution in a transition to a society where we get more renewable and sustainable energy sources. I hope that the government will find good pragmatic solutions, including: the power situation in central Norway, that the government can keep a focus on short-range power and, inter alia: take a positive view of the process that Industriekraft Møre is now working on. With regard to the insertion of the representative Marthinsen, the representative says that the Right is in agreement with the Advance Party on the requirement that gas power should not be discharged. What Mr Marthinsen forgets to say is quite important, namely that our position is that we should not require cleaning from day one. We also want to clean up, but we do not want to demand it from day one just to get up a larger and more flexible energy production.

It has that effect in any case, it distorts coal as long as coal is marginal in the power market – and it is. The conclusion of this debate is that the government defends that it has a fundamentally different treatment of equal actors. They require a technology that they themselves do not think is available. Norwegian energy policy is subject to national CO2 framework conditions instead of considering energy security as a national thing and climate policy as a global thing. It shows that no matter what Kristin Halvorsen tried to communicate five years ago that 15 years of Norwegian gas power struggle is over, it lives at best. Because immediately a mention is made of the word gas power plant in this room, it is completely blackened in the logical contexts of the government's energy policy, and one is unable to move on. Norwegian gas is good as long as you export it, but it's cruel when we use it in this country. It's a policy I'm taking away from, and I think the voters in 2013 will also take away from it.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e1467	Document 8:98 S is a proposal from the representatives of Ketil Solvik-Olsen, Henning Skumsvoll, Oskar Jarle Grimstad and Torkil Åmland to strengthen local land management in the coastal zone by amending Chapter 11 of the Law on Building Prohibition in the coastal zone and by limiting State authorities' right of objection and redress. It is not the first time such a proposal has been promoted and discussed, but the Stortinget has not previously endorsed similar proposals. It has been an important principle for many years that the beach zone should be accessible to all, and that one needs tools to withstand a constant pressure to build in the beach zone. It's probably a pressure that all of us who either live or spend a lot of time in coastal communities with fine archipelagos, etc., are very well acquainted with, and that is the subject of much political discussion at both local, regional and national level. In Norway there has been a legal ban on construction in the 100-metre belt along the sea since 1965. Unlike the recent signals from individual batches, we, the red-green batches, are concerned that the general interest is to be favoured in this 100 metre belt. There is reason to recall that the general construction ban in the 100-metre belt has been the subject of broad political agreement for a long time. There can therefore be no surprise on the right-hand side that the desire to care for the public interest has led to a narrowing of access to the exemption. The matter is really simple. People who do not own shall have access to the sea and the beaches. People who don't own should be able to have this access without feeling that they're doing something wrong. Our coast must not be walled again by the settlement that makes the beach zone inaccessible absolutely everywhere. It is important to take care of. One can sometimes get the impression in this debate that it is not the municipalities that are responsible for dealing with such matters, but it is. The fact that the Progress Party is still surprised that the statesmen are a contributing player in this work is therefore a little special. The task of the peoples is to take care of national interests for our common good in the coastal zone. The party of progress, which, in other contexts, would like to call for more state control, more central control, therefore has limited credibility when criticising today's system on a principle basis. The draft guidelines on the coastal zone have received many consultation opinions. There are many who have pointed out that the division of areas with large and smaller pressures should not follow the municipal boundaries. It's a video game the government follows up, and as the council has also said, it's a good objection. The same applies to the recordings from the 24 municipalities which state that they are incorrectly placed in the category of municipalities with a high pressure on the areas. Here both the Council of State and the Department have a sensible and listening attitude, and they need to take a closer look at the case of each municipality. It is worth reminding in this context that the Right in Government did not practice such a differentiation. The current current zeal for the liberalisation and privatisation of our common values is therefore a little poor, because we know from experience that the party is able to do so much better. As the position suggests, the majority, consisting of the red-green parties plus the Christian People's Party, are engaged in the work of ensuring good enforcement of the management of the beach zone, while respecting the need for differentiated administration between different parts of the country and differentiation within the municipalities. That is why we recommend not to approve the proposal.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e1490	The proposal that we are debating here today is a very good one in the sense that it reveals in a very good way the difference between the red-green government and the right side of Norwegian policy. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e1830	The Christian People's Party, as indicated in its position, stands together with the majority in this case – together with not supporting the proposal that has been promoted. There is also a question of a balance between today's population and the generations that come after us. It is also a fact that local democracy has its challenges in terms of spatial planning. It may be as simple as a local government figure in a municipality for a period wanting to expand the southern part of its coastal zone, while the local government in the period afterwards wants to expand the northern part of the coastal zone and have a different view of it. And that's how you can keep eating up a beach zone year by year, period by period. Therefore, there is, and should be, a balance between national policies and local autonomy in coastal zone issues. It is very important that there are national guidelines and that policy is used to ensure that future generations are taken into account.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e1877	The representative proposal on environmental and climate work in municipalities gives us the opportunity to focus on an important issue: the central role of municipalities in achieving the objectives of national environmental and climate work. The main aim of the proposals is to increase the development and exchange of competences in municipalities in order to enable them to meet national environmental and climate objectives. Proposals focus on the conservation of biological deficiency, soil protection, the conservation of open air areas, access to green lungs, the remediation of contaminated soil, local air pollution, waste and greenhouse gas emissions, and they believe that municipalities need to be better equipped to solve these tasks locally. The municipalities are involved in managing all nature in Norway. Local government shall contribute to the implementation of both local and national environmental policies. Local authorities have many roles and tools for e.g. bringing down greenhouse gas emissions in Norway. They're political actors, they're service providers, they're buyers, they're ownership owners, and they're responsible for good places to live for the people. Therefore, they can make a significant contribution to reducing greenhouse gas emissions both in their own operations by imposing strict requirements as purchasers and by influencing other actors. In particular, they can affect emissions from transport, waste landfills, stationary energy use and agriculture. Municipalities have many of the keys to achieve these goals. We need to raise awareness of natural disasters in order to understand the measures needed to achieve national and international objectives. As the proposers, the government is also busy that municipalities will be able to play that role. Therefore, the government has focused on precisely environmental and climate work in municipalities. In the setting, the following examples: Through the Future Programme, Norway and Norway are cooperating 13 major cities to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and make cities more environmentally friendly. Through exchange of experience with other cities and cooperation with both countries, businesses and citizens, cities will become greener. In «Citys of the Future», climate-friendly products are more commonplace and cleaner. In the programme «Livskraftige kommuner», 200 municipalities have worked in learning networks with other municipalities. The good advice and experience that will be exchanged is about everything from how to turn back giant bear biscuits, to waste management and energy efficiency improvement. The programme is linked to the Green Energy Municipalities project, which focuses on energy efficiency improvement and green energy use to reduce emissions. In addition, the Ministry of Local and Regional Affairs has financed climate and energy courses for municipalities since 2007. In addition, there is a set of means of training in the new Law on Planning and Building. The leadership role of the Directorates, county councillors and county councils towards the municipalities should be strengthened. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e1926	The right supports the Left's proposal. Norwegian municipalities need sufficient resources, knowledge and competence to ensure the important role of municipalities in environmental and climate work. The majority of Norwegian municipalities have it today. The largest municipalities have the opportunity to develop and follow up offensive, professionally anchored environmental and climate policies. Oslo is a good example of a large municipality with very ambitious climate targets and good results to point to. However, the Norwegian average municipality is small in number of inhabitants, but large in the number of square metres each of them can fasten. It makes it professional and resource-intensive to meet national objectives such as the conservation of biodiversity, soil protection, open air areas, decontamination of contaminated land, air pollution, waste management and greenhouse gas emissions. Most of the environmental challenges in Norway need to find their local solution. Therefore, it is essential that we enable the municipalities to do that job in a good way. Pending that municipal reform many of us may agree that is a prerequisite for strengthening and further developing local self-government, the proposals of the Left are correct and necessary. In Oslo, we see what can be done if the municipality has a resource and political priority for climate and environmental work. The greenhouse gas emissions shall be cut by 50 pst. by 2030. It is an ambitious objective that requires ambitious action. The 750 enterprises of the municipality are being certified for the environment. Oil firing will be phased out of all municipal buildings by 2012. Remote heat production has doubled and biogas and bio-fertilisation production is under construction, based on household food waste and sewage. On the Skøyen in Oslo there are 5,000 dwellings that get heat and hot water based on waste heat in the main sewer. It's probably not going to be much more circuit-based. In Bjørvika, fjord heating is to be established that will cool the buildings in the summer and heat them in the winter. Rivers are reopened. The fjord has never been cleaner than this year. The water in the spring is cleaned in a split new water treatment plant on Oset, and the land around the city is preserved on nature's premises ever since Christiania bought its first forests in the 1800s. With a growth of 10,000 to 15 000 people this year, Oslo practises an environmentally friendly consolidation policy around the collective node, not least illustrated by the denseness of Norway's largest collective node, Oslo S. It must be an important and proper priority to enable municipalities to identify local environmental challenges, whether they are contaminated land or water, waste management or natural habitat type registration. It is equally important to provide local authorities with tools to follow-up in practice. The person closest to the problem is often the most suitable person to solve it — and also with the municipalities in Norway. If we are to meet the objective of the climate agreement to cut emissions in Norway by 15-17 million tonnes of CO2 by 2020 – and I have made that quite clear here today that the Right is behind – we have to act on a broad front. Today it is the large municipalities that go ahead and show way on many important questions, while the state and the government are leaning after. We should give all the municipalities the opportunity to join in and pull the load.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e2097	It is important to anchor the past when changing for the future. That's what gives us identity and tells us who we are. These words, I think, sum up in a short but precise and good way what important tasks have been assigned to the national antiquities office. The Riksantikvar is given responsibility for the accumulation and government to preserve national cultural monuments. Through the law on cultural memories, the national antiquities have among other things: power to protect buildings and structures. These safeguard decisions and other decisions under the Cultural Memory Act follow the rules of the Administrative Act on alert, justification and right of appeal. Any decision may subsequently be appealed against to the Ministry of the Environment, which is the ultimate national antitrust product, and may therefore be subject to political judgement at the end of the day. It is therefore highly misleading when it is manufactured as if the national product is not subject to democratic control and political control. The Riksantikvaren also has the right of objection under the Planning and Building Act in respect of the municipality's spatial plans. This is an ordinary procedure. In this area, too, if local authorities and national authorities do not reach an agreement, the Ministry of the Environment is the one who makes a final decision. The Riksantikvaren mission is a heavy social responsibility. Ensuring values of national value requires strong instruments and could conflict with local interests. We have similar situations in a number of areas of society, nature conservation, beach protection — which we discussed earlier in the hall — agriculture, just to name a few. The Riksantikvar shall point out whether cultural historical reasons have not been respected. It is the task of the National Antiquities to do that, a job and a mission that the Norwegian government has given to the office. I think the ricksman does that job in an exemplary and good way. When the national product exercises its right of objection, it is in line with the mandate given by the Norwegian Storing. To disagree politically with one thing is an honest thing. However, seeking to punish a public office in order to do its job because of political disagreement is both impeccable and in frontier countries for what one believes should be an open and well-functioning democracy. For this reason, I have to say that I react quite strongly when I see that the Progress Party in the wake of some specific issues where they disagree is seeking to cut the rickshaw. Politicians who do not deal with criticism and differing views are, in my view, very poor decision-makers. We in the red-green believe that the ricksantikvar has a very important, if not always as popular job. As a nation, it is important to protect its history. Our story is our national anchoring on both good and bad. In this area, the protection of cultural memories is very central. A nation that cannot afford to take into account central cultural memories as a historical anchor for posterity will also become a poor nation over time. We in the red-green will build up under the fact that Norway will continue to be a rich nation of more than material values in the future. We will also protect our rich history and our common cultural anchoring. For this reason, we are grateful for the important job that the national antiquities are doing in our society. For this reason, we do not support the proposal under consideration here today.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e2348	The purpose of this provision, which came into force almost two years ago, was and is as follows: to ensure greater regional coordination of purchasing centre policies to strengthen existing urban and urban centres to avoid developments leading to urban sprawl and car dependence to contribute to environmentally friendly transport choices to limit greenhouse gas emissions, not least: The Regulation established that purchasing centres can only be established or extended in accordance with guidelines in approved county plans or district plans. In areas not covered by approved county plans or district sub-plans, purchasing centres of more than 3000 m2 of use will not be allowed until approved plan is available. This regulation has a duration of up to 10 years or until it is replaced by regional planning provisions under the Planning and Building Act. The national policy provision for purchasing centres shall ensure a better follow-up to the guidelines of the counties. It provides that, with legally binding effect, approved county and county plans are to be taken into account for the planning of municipalities. New building permits or framework permits may not be granted contrary to national policy provisions. The county council has been empowered to enforce the provision. The main challenge of our time is man-made climate change. If we are to solve this challenge, political decisions are required at many levels, in many areas and in many sectors. Land and transport planning is an important area for addressing climate challenges. Through active land-use planning, we set decades ahead of us the leadership of society and the transport needs that society will have in the future. Where large shopping centres are located, it is quite obvious that they are very central in this context. These included: In this context, we wanted a national policy provision for a more active policy in this area. The national policy guideline is an instrument to ensure better follow-up of national and regional objectives and guidelines for the location of major purchasing centres. The aim is to achieve a more coordinated localisation policy across municipal and county borders. This also gives greater predictability to neighbouring municipalities and builders in their planning work. President, I don't think the time is running, but I'm not going to take 5 minutes. Just so the President knows! In addition to reducing transport needs and thus climate emissions, the Regulation is an important contribution to a more sustainable and robust urban and urban development. Before the implementation of this regulation, a number of new purchasing centres were planning outside cities and agglomerations, despite the fact that this was not in line with regional plans. Our aim, on the other hand, was that larger purchasing centres should preferably be located near collective nodes and centrally in our cities, so that more people could get a shorter distance and more could travel environmentally friendly. Studies at the time the provision was planned showed that some shopping centres outside the cities had a car share of more than 95 p.p., while malls with a central location in the city could reach car shares up to 20 p.p.m. These were figures that were incompatible with the objective of reducing the use of passenger cars, which is absolutely necessary if we are to achieve our climate goals – and even more necessary when there is such great doubt about what, among other things. The right shall do so whenever they do not wish to clean gas power plants at all. We need a conscious and restrictive approach to the location of purchasing centres in order to dampen future traffic growth. With regard to the development and strengthening of existing urban and urban centres, it has often been that shopping centres located outside cities and agglomerations have been a challenge also for the centre trade and have led to reduced turnover and empty shopping facilities. It makes it very difficult to create attractive and vibrant cities and centers, which most of us just want. There are many important considerations to be taken into account and all of which demonstrate the need for the national policy guidelines for the establishment of new purchasing centres. The working party and the red-green government cannot see that these considerations have been weakened in the last two years, rather on the contrary. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e2451	In the largest cities today, car traffic accounts for more than half of CO2 emissions. In recent years, many cities and agglomerations have experienced an extreme increase in car traffic, increased air pollution and poorer availability for those who do not have a car. In Bergen it was e.g. in the period 1990-2007, a growth in car traffic of more than 70 pst. So we have to recognise that we have a challenge in achieving a more sustainable urban and transport pattern. Developments have been marked by high land and energy consumption, strong growth in car traffic, increases in local air pollution and a declining share of public transport. We don't want it like that anymore. This development must be reversed. It requires an active attitude towards urbanisation, a conscious strengthening of existing centres, main structures and important collective veins. It requires the end of the unplanned deployment of car-based purchasing centres, which are partly located outside the centre areas. It requires the implementation of coordination of land and transport planning. The aim of introducing national policy decisions for the purchasing centre was to avoid further development of more urban sprawl, car use and poorer availability for those who do not have a car. As a follow-up to the previous national policy decision on temporary stoppages for shopping centres outside cities and agglomerations, state plans and district plans have been developed with guidelines for the localisation of trade and service in order to tell all states. These are plans developed through extensive cooperation between county councils, county councillors, municipalities and the local business community, which are politically anchored. The national policy decisions of the Purchasing Authority state that the establishment of the Purchasing Authority shall only take place in accordance with the politically acceptable state plans with guidelines for trade in goods and service functions. There is therefore a record bomb by the Right and the Advance Party today when, in the comments on the case, they stress that local democracy must be heard and that the direction lines are a override. They are not. The national policy guidelines for the purchasing centre only take into account the measures that are contrary to what is agreed by the local populace, and are thus in contrast to a clarification of local democracy. The guidelines shall contribute to the effective adoption of local elected representatives, and shall be followed and respected by the development interests. Here I suspect that it is primarily the development interests and not the local democracy for which the Right Party and the Progress Party are spokespersons in this matter. Because it is true that many of the establishments have been involved, often because planned urban development has lost its impact on the fight against commercial, lucrative interests. Professional development companies speculate that exemptions will be granted from the political plans, and the pressure against the municipalities is high. The centre party will be clear that it is not the commercial interests and money to control urban and urban development, but the reverse. By means of plans defined by the population of the counties and municipalities, an environmentally friendly urban and urban development should be ensured. The national policy guidelines build on this. That's why we still need these direction lines. The right hand side and the Progress Party state that they think it's a paradox that the regulation will facilitate center-close trade, while at the same time it's a goal to limit car use in center-closed strokes. However, here is the point that one can precisely secure alternative transport services and facilitate collective provision, for pedestrians and not facilitate car-based communities by spreading the trade supply. It is 17 years since the Stortinget established national policy guidelines for coordinating land and transport planning. In these, the Storting stressed that congestion in the centre area and reduction in car traffic reduces land and energy demand. This provides safer, more pleasant, and more accessible outdoor areas. In my view, the Progress Party and the Right's proposal are contrary to this national governance principle, which has been the current objectives of land and environment policy since the 1970s. In this case, the Right and Advance Party praise the establishment of car-based purchasing centres outside the centre, and write that large purchasing centres outside the big cities will act as a «price correction». Whether that is the case, the question of what is most important for the Right and the Progress Party is increasing trade competition or ensuring an environmentally friendly and sustainable urban and urban development in Norway for the succeeding generations.

If not, taxi is the option. I agree that the car is necessary in many Norwegian districts, but the car is not a goal in itself. I found Skumsvoll pointing at a very important point, namely that there are a number of people who don't have a car and can't drive a car – older people, disabled people and others – and they're the ones who first of all would be the losers if we set up a system that leads to the big buyers establishing themselves outside the cities, at the expense of local shops in the urban centres where there is still a doctor's office, where there is a town hall, where there is an elder's office, etc. There may be a few buses in the Norwegian district, but the buses that are there are just going to the centres. Of course, it's a taxi offer, but it's a relatively expensive offer if you're going far from town to a mall and there's no local offer. So I would like to stress that this is not a debate for or against the malls and shops. There is a debate on what planning should take place in society in this area, and what guidelines should apply when planning for, for example, shops in connection with both the construction and the existing public transport services.

These include measures ranging from increased collective provision in six cities, main road networks for bicycles, eco-bicycling, efficiency of passenger cars, phase-in of gas ferries and shore-stream for regular ferries, energy management and increased use of bioenergy in industry, transition from fossil energy to other energy carriers in residential and industrial buildings, optimised fertiliser use in agriculture and increased use of fertilisers in forestry, to mention something. ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e2951	On 20 April the oil rig «Deepwater Horizon» exploded and sank in the Gulf of Mexico. The rig was operated by BP and was in full operation drilling an oil well at 1,544 meters deep on the Macondo field in the Gulf of Mexico. The rigging explosion claimed eleven lives and became the beginning of an oil disaster world has not seen its way to – and has not yet seen the outcome. The first time after the accident, BP informed me that the well leaked approximately. 5,000 barrels of oil a day. The estimates turned out to be too low, and BP was accused early of trying to inflate the magnitude of the disaster. The latest updated data indicate that the leakage rate is three to four times higher. Daily it leaks 15 000 to 20000 barrels of oil straight into the sea, or nearly 3 million. liters. What we've witnessed to this page is a tragic demonstration of almost total powerlessness on the part of the actors. I've traveled this interpellation because we also have to draw lessons from what's happening in the Gulf of Mexico. BP has done so far several attempts to stop the oil leak. The company has tried to close the safety valve, lower a so-called ‘top hat’ above the point of release to capture the oil, kill the well with oil sludge and eventually cut the riser tube and connect to the pipe outlet. Yesterday evening the first messages came that one might have succeeded in stopping a part of the discharge. The main measures taken are drilling of two relief wells that will release pressure and control the well. The drilling started on May 2 and 16, but the operation takes from three to six months. If the current state of play — or other measures — fails, emissions can continue until August–September. In retrospect, BP has acknowledged that the company was not prepared for oil leaks on deep water. BP Chief Tony Hayward recently said the following to the Financial Times newspaper: there's no doubt that we didn't have the tools you need for such an action. No one today is able to predict the long-term consequences of emissions from the accident. The oil silage is being cleaned for full length along the beaches and wetlands of the affected states. A research centre published Thursday last week data simulations showing that the oil can round the southern tip of Florida and frame the beaches along the west coast of the Land. Then the oil can spread to the entire east coast of the United States and the Atlantic within a few months. A thin layer of oil and balls with oil washed on Friday on the white beaches of Pensacola, the furthest west of Florida. At least 200 km of Louisiana's vulnerable coastline has already been severely hit, and more and more birds and animals are being found in the helpless state of the sea or swamps. In particular, 15 psts are caught in the Gulf. of the United States of America’s seafood production. The oil disaster in the Gulf of Mexico is now called Obama’s ‘Katrina’. The political game, and not least the disclaimer of responsibility of the responsible companies and the attempt to blame each other, has been a sad sight. During a hearing at the United States Congress on 11 May, the heads of BP, Transocean and Halliburton blamed the accident on each other. The US authorities have placed the responsibility with the operator BP and have sent a bill of 69 million. US dollars to BP to cover the expenditure that the government has so far incurred. BP has received a deadline of 1 July to pay. President Obama has taken criticism to be late on the track, especially in relation to oil protection and cleaning. Towards the end of May, the President came with a strong criticism of the bureaucrats in the Minerals Management Service, who are responsible for licensing and controlling the oil companies. The president announced a large-scale cleanup in the administration. He has also imposed a temporary ban on deep water exploration until 2011. The moratorium may include: impact on Statoil, which holds 448 licences in the Gulf of Mexico. Most of these licenses are on deep water. Statoil is already drilling at 2,000 meters deep. The oil disaster in the Gulf of Mexico is a strong warning of the risks associated with oil extraction under the seabed. 70 pst. the surface of the earth is covered by sea and sea, and discharges spread rapidly with the ocean currents over large areas. Even the biggest technology optimists have got something to think about after the accident in the Gulf of Mexico. Those who fight for a quick opening for oil exploration in our most vulnerable coastal spawning grounds for fish have a tough job ahead of them after what has happened in the Gulf of Mexico over the past six weeks. The disaster is also a powerful source of oil nation Norway, which has been producing oil on the continental shelf for over 30 years. Many suggest that Norway has come further than the United States in terms of its management and control of oil activity and safety requirements. When the United States is splitting up Minerals Management Service and tightening its grip on the oil industry, they see among other things. to Norway and want to learn from how we have chosen to organise our business. There's reason to be proud of it. Norwegian oil history is a unique success story. But the impotence we've seen in the last few weeks tells us with all manner of clarity that we're not invulnerable. Only a major oil disaster is needed to change this picture. Most of us remember the Bravo blast and the Alexander Kielland accident in the early phase of Norwegian oil history. Later, too, it has been close on several occasions. In parallel with the accident in the Gulf of Mexico, and in the shadow of the attention, the Gullfak C platform was evacuated on 19 May following a gas vent hazard caused by a pressure change in a well. It took Statoil a week to get control of the pressure situation in the well. This is the third case of serious incidents of less than six months at the Gullfak C platform. All the episodes have happened in the same well. Last case was little Christmas Eve. That episode was characterised by the Petroleum Monitoring Authority as the most serious incident on Norwegian pedestal in 2009. The last episode is called more serious. Petroleum activity on Norwegian base is changing. The simple wells have already been drilled. What remains are harder wells, many of them on deep water. The desire for increased extraction efficiency also challenges safety. With the exception of the Worm Lange, the deepest fields on the Norwegian base are less than 400 metres deep. In the years ahead, oil companies are going much deeper in Norwegian waters. Shell is currently drilling at 1,300 metres deep on the Gro field outside the Helgeland coast. Statoil has made findings on the Luva field west of the Sandness Lake that lies about as deep. Drilling on deep water makes greater demands both on drilling technology and on standby in case something should go wrong. Development of new technologies is more important than ever, but unfortunately, technological development does not have the same pressure as before. According to Technische Ukeblad nr. 20 for this year there have been several years since it was launched a pilot on a Norwegian base. This concerns both the Oil Directorate and the Petroleum Authority. The bear Breed in the directorate states the following to the leaf: ‘We are concerned that it has been a long time since pilot projects were carried out. OD believes that the industry needs to invest more in new technologies that can increase extraction and improve safety.’ A type of drilling technology that is widely researched and which increases safety is so-called pressure-balanced drilling. For the time being, it has been used on a Norwegian base to a small extent. The environmental movement, on its part, has also called for a deep water drilling stop in Norway until the causes of the disaster in the Gulf of Mexico have been fully investigated. According to Dagens Näringsliv, oil professor Øystein Noreng at Handelshöchskolan BI also supports such a claim. I do not want for my part to conclude on such a question. However, I expect the professional authorities to assess this carefully. In the evening post of 3 June, the Minister of Labour rejected the question of drilling stop and indicated that the Petroleum Authority has made a concrete assessment of the Gro drilling and found that there is no basis for drawing a similar conclusion in Norway as in the United States. On the same day that the Minister was out with this message in the evening post, the Petroleum Authority organised its security conference ‘Always prepared?’ on how to draw lessons from major accidents. The conference was originally supposed to indicate that it was 30 years since the ‘Alexander Kielland’ accident, but in the last few years got a more up-to-date profile. Stavanger Aftenblad covered the conference and drew the following quotation from the lecture to the supervisor Magne Ognedal: «Kunne Deepwater Horizon happened in Norway and could Kielland happen again? The answer to that is yes. We have to assume that there is a risk associated with petroleum activities. The risk picture tries to draw in the management plans. It becomes a political matter whether or not the risk picture is acceptable’. I agree that the task of the authorities is to control the business and clarify the degree of risk. It's our job as politicians to make the final decisions. The disaster in the Gulf of Mexico has shown that marine oil protection can never be anything but a harm reduction tool. There is currently no oil protection equipment to ensure the protection of vulnerable natural areas in the sea or along the coast. Nor does there exist any equipment that is dimensioned to take away such quantities of oil as a real oil disaster entails. The ‘Full City’ wreck outside Langesund last summer was a real demonstration in that way. In this way, oil protection will never be sufficient to offset the potential risk of offshore oil activities in harsh weather conditions. We politicians must be aware of this also when we are about to decide on the possible opening of new areas for oil activities in vulnerable northern sea areas. I have therefore wanted to focus on this issue with a debate in Stortinget. I want to know how Norway is equipped to deal with an oil disaster, what we can learn from the situation in the Gulf of Mexico, and how the government will work on a systematic review of the experience of this terrible disaster, so that we can be as equipped to deal with our own challenges as best we can.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e3006	I want to start by being allowed to thank the interpellant for raising this issue. A whole world is now following the battle in the Gulf of Mexico to stop the oil spill after the Deepwater Horizon exploded and sank on April 20, 2010. Eleven people disappeared in the depths, and several were injured. In addition, oil spills have still not been stopped. We do not yet know the overall extent of the disaster, but we know that the consequences are already very extensive, both human, environmental and economic. The fatal event is an important reminder of the importance of a high level of HMS in the petroleum industry, and I have assured myself that all actors in Norwegian petroleum activities are closely following the review given that all relevant learning is to be done. The causes of the major accident in the Gulf of Mexico have not been identified, but in order to be able to follow developments here in Norway, the Petroleum Authority has established an internal project group that follows up and assesses the measures taken in view of their relevance to the activities on Norwegian pedestal. Similar activities are carried out by other relevant authorities. The Land Association of the Oil Industry has informed that they are engaged in similar work. The aim is and must be to prevent such a thing from happening in Norway. The representative refers to the Gullfaks C event that is now ongoing in the North Sea. This is a serious incident in which one of the two barriers ensuring control of the well is weakened. However, I would like to stress that there is no reason in any way to equate this major accident with that in the Gulf of Mexico. The exhaust valve is intact and intensive work is being done to create barrier No. 2 and secure the well. Petroleum monitoring closely follows up Statoil, and the Petroleum Control Authority assesses Statoil’s control of the situation. Following the 1980 ‘Alexander Kielland’ accident, there has been a public demand that the establishment be operated with the lowest possible risk of damage and accidents. The regulatory and supervisory responsibilities for the activities are divided into several departments and underlying agencies. The Ministry of Labour, of Petroleum Control, is responsible for the legislation designed to prevent such incidents and for the identification of cause and effect. The Ministry of Fisheries and Coastal Affairs, at the Coastal Works, is responsible for state preparedness for acute pollution, including oil spill collection during state action. The Ministry of the Environment, at the Directorate for Climate Change and Pollution, requires preparedness to deal with acute pollution in the petroleum industry. The Ministry of Oil and Energy, at the Directorate of Oil, is responsible for resource management. The oil industry operates under natural and technological conditions, which means that faults and accidents have the potential for serious damage to humans, the environment and material values. To meet this situation, a comprehensive HMS code has been developed that imposes stringent safety and management requirements. The regulatory framework is supported by industry standards. Standards have been developed following thorough processes for the qualification of new technologies and experience learning. Petroleum supervision ensures a close follow-up of the industry. The government's explicit expectation to the petroleum industries is that it will be world leader in the HMS. The HMS Code is based on the systematic accountability of all operators in the industry. The operator is directly responsible for the compliance of subcontractors. Through risk analyses and impact assessments, actors shall acquire the best possible knowledge of the risks of their activities. Where the activity is taking place in an environmentally vulnerable area, the choice of technology, organisation and preparedness needs to be adapted to compensate for increased risks to the environment. Barriers that are independent shall always be used so that a single fault cannot lead to an accident. It is a fundamental prerequisite for actors to work towards an ever-increasing improvement in the overall level of safety, and the learning of their own experience is an important tool here. The Authority’s oversight is in addition to its own control and aims at ensuring that the responsible companies carry out preventive activities that are proportionate to the potential impact on the environment. To the question of whether Norway is equipped to deal with an oil disaster, it is important to stress that the main focus is on preventing such a disaster. Nevertheless, it is important to recognise that it can never be guaranteed that a major accident cannot occur. Therefore, in addition to the strict requirements for the planning and operation of the establishment, a number of requirements for the establishment and exercise of preparedness have also been set out in the legislation. The main objective of the legislation is to establish measures to prevent or limit the consequences of accidents or accidents. Oil protection equipment itself is the responsibility of the operators and must be coordinated under the Petroleum Act, including with public emergency facilities. The coastal facility, which is responsible for state preparedness for acute oil or chemical emissions, may take over the management of an oil protection operation related to a specific incident if deemed necessary. Of course, if there is a major incident, the concern for life and health will come first, but the work on collecting oil and combating oil pollution will start as early as possible. In the oil protection operation, private, i.e. oil companies, state and municipal and intermunicipal readiness will be connected. Under the law, the State has the power to convene municipal emergency response resources and resources from private operators with emergency response obligations. Where there is a risk of very significant pollution damage, any person may be instructed to make available material or personnel to combat the accident. Oil management forecasts will be developed by the Meteorological Institute with the help of the Marine Research Institute, which, together with the Directorate of Fisheries, will also provide advice related to the allocation and presence of vulnerable marine living resources. In other words, such a situation would require coordination of all public and private resources and would require the management of the government's crisis selection. Mechanical collection is the priority measure, but it depends on weather conditions and is not effective at too high waves. Chemicals to speed up the natural breakdown of oil at sea could be used, depending on the type of oil, the time of the event and the possible occurrence of an event. Both the time and the location of the event on the Norwegian base will be of importance for the management of the oil protection operation. Finally, I would like to stress that, on the part of the authorities, we are following developments in the Gulf of Mexico very closely with a view to learning as much as possible. The follow-up of the accident in the Gulf of Mexico will be carried out in a coordinated manner with a view to updating the management plan for the Barents Sea and the sea areas outside the Lofoten. I will also present new knowledge and the possible implementation of changes as a result of the accident in the Gulf of Mexico in the forthcoming Interim Communication on Working Conditions, the Working Environment and Safety. On Friday, the oil and energy minister and I will have a meeting with the major oil companies to hear their assessments of the current situation and what they are doing. I consider the ongoing need to take further action to ensure learning from this event.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-06-07.ana.ud1163e3066	The oil disaster in the Gulf of Mexico has made a very strong impression on most of us. The interpellant travels for that reason a very important theme in its interpellation that we are dealing with here today.
