The TV images from that event as well as the eyewitness accounts from the survivors involved in retrospect have made a huge impression on all of us. The accident 30 years ago reminds us all of how fundamental it is to make tough demands on health, the environment and safety towards those who operate on Norwegian pedestals, and I believe that we have done so as decision-makers – both before and now. Every time we deal with concrete development cases on a Norwegian base here in Stortinget, the concern for health, the environment and safety is what we are primarily concerned about. Unfortunately, adverse events occur. Fortunately, although we have been proud of several incidents such as the ‘Alexander Kielland’ accident, we can never guard against adverse events – despite the fact that in Norway we are at the forefront of making tough demands on health, the environment and the safety of our operations on our pedestal, precisely to prevent adverse events. Because we can never guard ourselves by a hundred percent, we also have to make demands for an oil protection device that is as good as possible. If the worst happens, we have to prepare ourselves to deal with it in such a way that the adverse effects are minimised in terms of the environmental impact. Many thousands of people live large parts of their lives in or in close proximity to Norwegian pedestal. These people are entitled to something as fundamental as a safe and secure workplace. This responsibility lies with the employer, but it is also our job as politicians to ensure that we make and develop demands at all times, so that this basic need is satisfied. I believe that this responsibility is our responsibility as politicians. But it is equally important that all those who live along the coast and have their jobs there, both in fisheries, tourism and other industries, should also have basic security so that we can make the most stringent demands and have the best security and preparedness in place so that we can live and work along the coast. In order to ensure compliance with our requirements for standards in the HMS area, it is the responsibility of the Petroleum Authority to have the daily responsibility for this. Petroleum supervision is a strong and independent industry surveillance for the working environment and safety, including major accident risks. Petroleum supervision is the daily responsibility for a close follow-up of the industry to which the Council of State was committed. Our political task will therefore be to ensure that, in the future, the Petroleum Control Authority will continue to be a strong and independent oversight capable of carrying out its tasks in the best possible way. As for what we can learn from the event in the Gulf of Mexico, it's probably early on to answer that question now, with the exception of the reminder that it can actually happen unforeseeable. Until now, the Americans have had their focus on stopping or managing emissions, which I think we're all going to be happy for. Then we're going to hope that they'll succeed with this soon. For the red-green and for the government, the priority of health, environment and safety on Norwegian soil is first priority. For this reason, our ambitions are to make the world's most demanding demands in these areas, and at all times make use of the foremost technology in this area. But I have to say that the Gulf of Mexico also shows the importance of acquiring knowledge about both the offshore resource base and the natural values before a start-up operation on a Norwegian base. A broadest possible information and knowledge should be the basis for decision-making before we make our choices here to land. The experience and cause of the situation in the Gulf of Mexico will be important for us, and it can also form the basis for increased demands here to land. I have to say that I share the view of the interpellant Høybråten, and also President Obama's view, which is not only concerned, but has very clear speech against the company operating in the Gulf of Mexico, which is trying to blame others and cover up what has happened. No one should do after them.
